Y\cdot\mathscr{F}:=\frac{(T_{\mathfrak{q}_{0}}-C(\mathfrak{q}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi^{c}}))X}{C(\mathfrak{q}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi})-C(\mathfrak{q}_{0},\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi^{c}})}\mathscr{F};\quad\varepsilon_{i}\cdot\mathscr{F}=(\phi\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}_{i}}^{-1})U_{p}-1)\mathscr{F}.

Put
\mathscr{F}_{3}=\mathscr{H}_{3}\,(\mbox{mod }{X^{r_{A}+1}})
. One verifies easily that

\displaystyle Y\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}

\displaystyle=Y\cdot\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi}\equiv X\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi}\,(\mbox{mod }{X^{r_{A}+1}});

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}\dots\varepsilon_{r}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv-A\cdot\theta_{\phi}^{(\Sigma_{p})}\equiv-a^{*}\cdot X^{r_{A}}\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi}\,(\mbox{mod }{X^{r_{A}+1}}).

One verifies that

X^{r_{B}}\mathscr{F}_{3}=(1+\frac{A}{B})Y^{r_{B}}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3},\text{ and }\varepsilon_{1}\dots\varepsilon_{r}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}=-a^{*}Y^{r_{A}}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}.

Setting
z_{3}=1+\frac{A}{B}\in\Lambda_{P}
, it follows that
\mathscr{H}_{3}
is annihilated by the ideal

(6.15)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle:=I_{r_{A},r_{B},z_{3},a^{*}}

\displaystyle=(Y^{r_{A}+1},\varepsilon_{i}^{2},X\varepsilon_{i},Y\varepsilon_{i},Y(X-Y),X^{r_{B}}-z_{3}Y^{r_{B}},\varepsilon_{1}\dots\varepsilon_{r}+a^{*}Y^{r_{A}}).

By a similar argument as in the case (II), one can show that
\left\{X^{i}\mathscr{F}_{3}\right\}_{i=0}^{r_{B}-1}
,
\left\{Y^{j}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}\right\}_{j=1}^{r_{A}-1}
and
\left\{\varepsilon_{J}\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}\right\}_{J\neq\emptyset}
are linearly independent and conclude that
W\cdot\mathscr{F}_{3}
is free of rank one over
W
. By (
5.3
), we also verify that

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{l}}\mathscr{F}_{3}=

\displaystyle(\phi\Psi_{3}({\rm Fr}_{\mathfrak{L}})+\tau_{K/F}(\mathfrak{l})\phi\Psi_{3}({\rm Fr}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{L}}}}))\cdot\mathscr{H}_{3},

where
\Psi_{3}:G_{K,S}\to W^{\times}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(\sigma)=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{c}(\sigma)+\frac{\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\sigma)-\boldsymbol{\Psi}^{c}(\sigma)}{X}Y.

Applying Proposition
6.1
to
\lambda
with
\widetilde{\Psi}=\Psi_{3}
,
Z=X-Y
and
\psi=\ell^{\rm ac}
, we obtain

-a^{*}Y^{r_{A}}+\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}(X-Y)^{r}\equiv 0\,(\mbox{mod }{(X-Y)^{r}\mathfrak{m}_{W}}).

Since
r_{A}>r_{B}
, we have
Z^{r_{B}}=(X-Y)^{r_{B}}=\frac{a^{*}}{b^{*}}Y^{r_{A}}\in W
.
This implies that
r_{B}\geq r
and that
\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}=0
if
r_{B}>r
and
\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}=b^{*}
if
r_{B}=r
.
This finishes the proof in all cases.
∎

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that (

F

-Leo
) and (

Σ

-Leo
) hold. Then we have

{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=0}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,-s,\chi)\geq r

and

\lim_{s\to 0}\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,-s,\chi)}{s^{r}}=\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}\backslash\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})).

Proof.
.

Let
B(s):=\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{s}(B)
be the meromorphic function on
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
. Then
B(s)/s^{r}|_{s=0}=B/X^{r}|_{X=0}
based on the choice of the uniformizer
X
. By Proposition
5.3
,

(6.16)

\displaystyle B(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{s}}\cdot\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,-s,\chi)}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{*}(s,\chi)\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}\backslash\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))}\cdot h_{K/F}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\zeta_{F,p}(1-s)\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{s}({\rm Fr}_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}))}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,0,{\mathbf{1}})\left<{\Delta_{K}{\mathrm{N}}{\mathfrak{C}}_{\chi}}\right>^{s}}\cdot{\rm Ex}_{\mathfrak{l}_{0}}(s).

To simply the notation, we put

{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(s,\chi):={\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{*}(s,\chi)\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}\backslash\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})).

Combined with Proposition
6.3
, Proposition
4.8
and
{\rm Ex}_{\mathfrak{l}_{0}}(0)=1
, we obtain

\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}=\frac{B(s)}{s^{r}}\Big|_{s=0}=\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,-s,\chi)}{s^{r}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(s,\chi)}\Big|_{s=0}.

The theorem follows.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

Suppose that the Leopoldt hypotheses (

F

-Leo
) and (

Σ

-Leo
) hold and that
(\mathfrak{f},p\mathfrak{d}_{K/F})=1
. Suppose that
r_{\Sigma}(\chi)>0
and
\mathfrak{P}_{1}\in\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}
. Then we have
L_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)=0
and

\frac{L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)}{s}\Big|_{s=0}=\mathscr{L}_{\chi}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}\backslash\left\{\mathfrak{P}_{1}\right\}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})).

Proof.
.

Let
r=r_{\Sigma}(\chi)
. Since
\eta_{\Sigma}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}})=-\log_{p}(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}))
for
\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}
,
by Theorem
4.9
we see that
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=0}({\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,0,\chi))=r
and if
r=1
and
\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}=\left\{\mathfrak{P}_{1}\right\}
, then

\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,0,\chi)}{s}\Big|_{s=0}=\eta_{\Sigma}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}})\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(0,\chi).

This implies that
\frac{L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)}{s}|_{s=0}=0
if
r>1
in view of Theorem
6.4
. Now suppose that
r=1
. Note that the cyclotomic logarithm
\ell^{\rm cyc}=-\eta_{\Sigma}-\eta_{\overline{\Sigma}}
, so if
r=1
and
\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}=\left\{\mathfrak{P}_{1}\right\}
, then

\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}=2\eta_{\Sigma}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}})-\mathscr{L}_{\chi}.

It follows that the cyclotomic derivative
\frac{L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)}{s}\Big|_{s=0}
equals

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,s,\chi)}{s}\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,0,\chi)-{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(s,-s,\chi)}{s}\Big|_{s=0}

\displaystyle=2\eta_{\Sigma}(\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}){\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(0,\chi)-\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(s,\chi)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\chi}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{**}(0,\chi).

This completes the proof of our main theorem.
∎

6.3.
Non-vanishing of
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)

We study the non-vanishing of
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)
via the one-sided divisibility of Iwasawa main conjecture for CM fields in
[
Hsi14a
]
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Let
\chi
be a non-trivial ray class character of prime-to-
p
order. Suppose that
(\chi,K,p)
satisfies the assumptions (
H1
) and (
H2
) in the introduction. If the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture (

Σ

-Leo
) is valid, then
{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(0,\chi)\neq 0
.

Proof.
.

This can be deduced from the one-sided divisibility result in Iwasawa main conjecture for the improved Katz
p
-adic
L
-function
{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{*}(\chi)
. To begin with, let
K_{\infty}
be the
\mathbf{Z}_{p}^{d+1}
-extension of
K
and for any
K_{\infty}/L/K
, put
\Lambda(L):={\mathcal{W}}\llbracket{\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L/K)}\rrbracket
. If
L_{2}\subset L_{1}
, then let
\Lambda(L_{1})\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda(L_{2})
be the surjection induced by
\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L_{1}/K)\to\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L_{2}/K)
. Let
S
be the union of the set of finite places of
K
where
\chi
is ramified and the set of places above
p
. Define the universal character
\boldsymbol{\chi}:G_{K,S}\to\Lambda(K_{\infty})^{\times},\,\,\sigma\mapsto\chi(\sigma)[\sigma]
. Let
\Lambda(L)^{*}=\mathop{\rm Hom}\nolimits_{cts}(\Lambda(L),\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p})
be the Pontryagin dual of
\Lambda(L)
endowed with
\Lambda(L)
-module structure given by
\lambda\cdot f(x)=f(\lambda x)
for
\lambda,x\in\Lambda(L)
. Let
\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda(L)^{*}
be the discrete
\Lambda(L)
-module
\Lambda(L)^{*}
with the Galois action via the character
\boldsymbol{\chi}
. Define the Selmer group

Sel_{L}(\chi):=\mathop{\rm ker}\nolimits\left\{{\rm H}^{1}(K_{S}/K,\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda(L)^{*})\to\prod_{w\in S\backslash\Sigma_{p}}{\rm H}^{1}(I_{w},\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda(L)^{*})^{D_{w}}\right\},

where
I_{w}
and
D_{w}
denote the inertia and decomposition groups of
w
. Then
Sel_{L}(\chi)
are discrete and co-finitely generated
\Lambda(L)
-modules. Let
{\rm char}_{\Lambda(L)}Sel_{L}(\chi)
be the characteristic ideal of the Pontryagin dual
Sel_{L}(\chi)^{*}
in
\Lambda(L)
.

Let
L
be the compositum of the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension and
K_{\Sigma_{p}}
. Since
{\rm H}^{0}(K,\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda(K_{\infty})^{*})=\left\{0\right\}
and
L
contains the cyclotomic
\mathbf{Z}_{p}
-extension, we have

(6.17)

Sel_{K_{\infty}}(\chi)^{*}\otimes_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}\Lambda(L)\sim Sel_{L}(\chi)^{*}

is pseudo-isomorphic of finitely generated
\Lambda(L)
-modules in view of
[
Hsi14a
, Remark 8.4]
. Let
I
be the kernel of the natural quotient map
\Lambda(L)\to\Lambda(K_{\Sigma_{p}})=\Lambda_{K}
. We have the exact sequence

0\to Sel_{K_{\Sigma}}(\chi)\to Sel_{L}(\chi)[I]\overset{{\rm loc}_{\overline{\Sigma}_{p}}}{\longrightarrow}\prod_{w\in\overline{\Sigma}_{p}}\left({\rm H}^{0}(I_{w},\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda(L)^{*})\otimes_{\Lambda(L)}\Lambda_{K}\right)^{D_{w}}.

Taking Pontryagin dual of the above exact sequence, we find that

(6.18)

\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}\frac{\Lambda_{K}}{(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}})}\to Sel_{L}(\chi)^{*}\otimes_{\Lambda(L)}\Lambda_{K}\to Sel_{K_{\Sigma}}(\chi)^{*}\to 0.

Recall that
[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}]\in\Lambda_{K}
is the image of the Frobenius at
\overline{\mathfrak{P}}
in
\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(K_{\Sigma_{p}}/K)\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{K}
and
[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}]\neq 1
by the ordinary assumption. The equation (
6.18
) implies that

\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}])\cdot{\rm char}_{\Lambda_{K}}(Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*})\subset{\rm char}_{\Lambda_{K}}(Sel_{L}(\chi)^{*}\otimes_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}\Lambda_{K}).

Combined with (
6.17
), we get

\displaystyle\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}])\cdot{\rm char}_{\Lambda_{K}}(Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*})\subset

\displaystyle{\rm char}_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}(Sel_{K_{\infty}}(\chi)^{*}\otimes_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}\Lambda_{K})

\displaystyle\subset

\displaystyle{\rm char}_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}(Sel_{K_{\infty}}(\chi)^{*})\otimes_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}\Lambda_{K}.

On the other hand, by
[
Hsi14a
, Theorem 2]
,

{\rm char}_{\Lambda(K_{\infty})}(Sel_{K_{\infty}}(\chi)^{*})\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\Lambda(K_{\infty}).

By Theorem
4.9
, it follows that

\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}])\cdot{\rm char}_{\Lambda_{K}}(Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*})\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\Lambda_{K}=\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\chi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})[\mathop{\rm Fr}\nolimits_{\overline{\mathfrak{P}}}])\cdot{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\Lambda_{K},

and hence

(6.19)

{\rm char}_{\Lambda_{K}}(Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*})\subset{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\Lambda_{K}.

Note that

{\rm H}^{0}(I_{w},\boldsymbol{\chi}\otimes\Lambda_{K}^{*})\otimes\Lambda_{K}/(X)=\left\{0\right\}\text{ for all }w\in S\backslash\Sigma_{p}.

By
[
Hsi14a
, Lemma 8.3]
, this implies that

(6.20)

Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*}\otimes_{\Lambda_{K}}\Lambda_{K}/(X)\simeq{\rm H}^{1}_{(\emptyset,f)}(K,\chi\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{p}}\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p})^{*}

By the Poitou-Tate duality,
{\rm H}^{1}_{(0,f)}(K,\chi^{-1}(1))
and
{\rm H}^{1}_{(\emptyset,f)}(K,\chi\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p})^{*}
have the same rank, so the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture implies that
{\rm H}^{1}_{(\emptyset,f)}(K,\chi\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{p}/\mathbf{Z}_{p})
is a finite group. Therefore, from (
6.20
), we deduce that the characteristic ideal of
Sel_{K_{\Sigma_{p}}}(\chi)^{*}
is not divisible by
X
, and by (
6.19
),

{\mathcal{L}}^{*}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\,(\mbox{mod }{X})={\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}^{*}(0,\chi)\neq 0.

This completes the proof.
∎

The following is an immediate consequence of Corollary
6.5
and Proposition
6.6
.

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
\chi
be a non-trivial anticyclotomic ray class character of
K
such that

(1)

(\chi,K,p)
satisfies the assumptions (
H1
) and (
H2
).

(2)

(

F

-Leo
) and (

Σ

-Leo
) both hold.

Then

{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=0}L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)=1\text{ if and only if }r_{\Sigma}(\chi)=1\text{ and }\mathscr{L}_{\chi}\neq 0.

Remark 6.8
.

One can also deduce the one side implication that
if
r_{\Sigma}(\chi)=1\text{ and }\mathscr{L}_{\chi}\neq 0
, then
{\mathrm{ord}}_{s=0}L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)=1
from
[
BS21
, Theorem 3.27]
combined with
[
Hsi14a
, Theorem 2]
and the existence of the trivial zeros

7.
Explicit calculations in the Rankin-Selberg convolution

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
5.3
.

7.1.
Adelic Hilbert modular forms

Let
k
be any integer. Let
\omega
be a Hecke character of
F
of finite order. Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\omega)
denote the space of automorphic forms of weight
k
and central character
\omega
, consisting of smooth and slowly increasing functions
\varphi:\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F)\backslash\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})\to\mathbf{C}
such that

\varphi(gu_{\infty}z)=\omega(z)\varphi(g)J(u_{\infty},\sqrt{-1})^{-k\Sigma},\quad z\in\mathbf{A}_{F}^{\times},\,u_{\infty}\in\mathop{\rm SO}\nolimits_{2}(F\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{R}).

Let
{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{\underline{k}}(\omega)\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{k}}(\omega)
be the subspace of cusp forms. For any integral ideal
\mathfrak{n}
of
F
, put

U_{1}(\mathfrak{n})=\left\{g=\begin{pmatrix}{a}&{b}\\
{c}&{d}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\widehat{\mathfrak{o}})\mid c\in\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathfrak{o}},\,d-1\in\mathfrak{n}\widehat{\mathfrak{o}}\right\}.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\omega)
be the subspace of
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\omega)
right invariant by
U_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
. For any
\varphi\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\omega)
, the Whittaker function
W_{\varphi}:\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})\to\mathbf{C}
of
\varphi
is defined by

W_{\varphi}(g):=\int_{F\backslash\mathbf{A}_{F}}\varphi(\begin{pmatrix}{1}&{x}\\
{0}&{1}\end{pmatrix}g)\boldsymbol{\psi}_{F}(-x)\mathrm{d}x.

To any Hilbert modular form
\mathbf{f}\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\omega)
, we can associate a unique automorphic form in
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\omega)
such that
for every
y\in(F\otimes\mathbf{R})^{\times}
and
a\in\widehat{F}^{\times}
, we have

(7.1)

W_{\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}{ya}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\right)=C(a\mathfrak{o},\mathbf{f})\cdot\left|ya\right|^{\frac{k}{2}}e^{-2\pi\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits(y)}\mathbb{I}_{(F\otimes\mathbf{R})_{+}}(y).

On the other hand, a Hilbert modular form
\mathbf{f}
is given by an
h
-tuple

\mathbf{f}=(f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}},\dots f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{h}}),\quad f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}}\in M_{k}(\mathbf{c}_{i},U_{1}(\mathfrak{n})).

Then we have

f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}}(\tau)=y^{-\frac{k\Sigma}{2}}\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}\left(\begin{pmatrix}{y}&{x}\\
{0}&{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}_{i}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\right)({\mathrm{N}}{\mathfrak{c}}_{i})^{\frac{k}{2}}\quad(\tau=x+\sqrt{-1}y)\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{F}).

Then

C(\beta{\mathfrak{c}}_{i},\mathbf{f})=c_{\beta}(f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}}),\quad C_{i}(0,\mathbf{f})=c_{0}(f_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{i}}).

If
\mathbf{f}\in S_{k}(\mathfrak{n},\omega)
is a Hilbert cuspidal newform of conductor
\mathfrak{n}
, then denote by
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
the unitary and irreducible automorphic representation of
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
generated by
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}
.
This representation
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
is discrete series at all infinite places and has conductor
\mathfrak{n}
and central character
\xi
.
Moreover,
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}
is the new vector in
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
and

(7.2)

L(s,\pi_{\mathbf{f}})=\Gamma_{\mathbf{C}}(s+k-1)^{[F:\mathbf{Q}]}D(s+\frac{k-1}{2},\mathbf{f}),

where
L(s,\pi_{\mathbf{f}})
is the automorphic
L
-function associated with
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
by Jacquet and Langlands.

7.2.
Eisenstein series

The aim of this subsection is to realize the weight
k
specialization
\nu_{k}({\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l}))
of the
\Lambda
-adic Eisenstein series
{\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l})
as adelic Eisenstein series in
[
Jac72
, Chapter V]
. For each place
v
, let
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F_{v}}
be the local component of the Teichmüller character
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F}
at
v
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the pair

\mathcal{D}=(k,\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0}),

where
k
is a positive integer, and
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathfrak{m}_{0}
are integral ideals of
F
such that
(\mathfrak{m},p\mathfrak{o})=1
and
(\mathfrak{m}_{0},p\mathfrak{d}_{F})=1
. Define

\Phi_{\mathcal{D}}=\otimes_{v\in\Sigma}\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}\otimes_{v\in{\bf h}}^{\prime}\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}\in{\mathcal{S}}(\mathbf{A}_{F}^{2})
by

•

\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}(x,y)=2^{-k}(x+\sqrt{-1}y)^{k}e^{-\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})}
if
v\mid\infty
,

•

\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}(x,y)=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{m}\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(x)\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{d}_{F_{v}}^{-1}}(y)
if
v\nmid p\mathfrak{m}_{0}\infty
,

•

\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}(x,y)=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{m}\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(x)\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times}}(y)
if
v\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
,

•

\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}(x,y)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F_{v}}^{-k}(x)\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times}}(x)\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(y)
if
v\mid p
.

Let
f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s}=\otimes_{v}f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s}\in I(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F}^{k},{\mathbf{1}},s)
, where

(7.3)

f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s}(g)=\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F_{v}}^{k}(\det g)\left|\det g\right|_{v}^{s+{\frac{1}{2}}}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}((0,t_{v})g)\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F_{v}}^{k}(t_{v})\left|t_{v}\right|_{v}^{2s+1}\mathrm{d}^{\times}t_{v}.

Consider the adelic Eisenstein series
E_{\mathbf{A}}(g,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},s}})
defined by (
4.3
). It is easy to verify that
E_{\mathbf{A}}(g,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},s}})
is an automorphic form in
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F}^{k})
whenever it is defined.
For
\tau=x+\sqrt{-1}y\in{\mathfrak{H}}_{F}
and
{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathbf{c}\mathfrak{o}
for
\mathbf{c}\in\widehat{F}^{(p\mathfrak{m}){\times}}
, put

E_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau):=y^{-\frac{k\Sigma}{2}}E_{\mathbf{A}}(\begin{pmatrix}{y}&{x}\\
{0}&{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s})|_{s=\frac{1-k}{2}}\cdot\left|\mathbf{c}\right|^{-\frac{k}{2}}.

Let
{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0})=(E_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{1}},\dots E_{{\mathfrak{c}}_{h_{F}^{+}}})\in M_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F}^{k})
. This way
E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},s}})|_{s=\frac{1-k}{2}}
is the automorphic form associated with
{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0})
via the relation (
7.1
). For any integer
n
coprime to
p
, put
\left<{n}\right>:=n\cdot\boldsymbol{\omega}((n))
.

Proposition 7.1
.

For
k\geq 2
, we have

\nu_{k}({\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0}))=\frac{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}\cdot{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0}).

Proof.
.

As in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, the
\beta
-th Fourier coefficient of
E_{\mathfrak{c}}
is given by

c_{\beta}(E_{\mathfrak{c}})=\prod_{v\in{\bf h}}W_{\beta}(\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}_{v}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s})\left|\mathbf{c}_{v}\right|_{v}^{-\frac{k}{2}}.

Let
v
be a prime
\mathfrak{l}
. Put
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}^{-1}\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}}\right>^{k}
. If
\mathfrak{l}\nmid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, then it is easy to see that

\displaystyle W_{\beta}(\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}_{v}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s})|_{s=\frac{1-k}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left|\mathbf{c}_{v}\right|_{v}^{\frac{k}{2}}\sum^{v(\beta\mathbf{c}_{v})}_{i=v(\mathfrak{m})}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{i}\text{ if }\mathfrak{l}\nmid p\mathfrak{m}_{0},

\displaystyle W_{\beta}(1,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(\beta)\text{ if }\mathfrak{l}\mid p.

For
\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle W_{\beta}(1,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s})=

\displaystyle\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{m}\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(t)\widehat{\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times}}}(-\beta t^{-1})\boldsymbol{\omega}^{k}(t)\left|t\right|_{v}^{2s}\mathrm{d}^{\times}t.

Since
\widehat{\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times}}}=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}}-\left|\varpi_{v}\right|_{v}\mathbb{I}_{\varpi_{v}^{-1}\mathfrak{o}_{v}}
, we find that

\displaystyle W_{\beta}(1,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s})=

\displaystyle\sum^{v(\beta)}_{i=v(\mathfrak{m})}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{F}^{k}(\varpi_{v}^{i})\left|\varpi_{v}^{i}\right|_{v}^{1-k}-\left|\varpi_{v}\right|_{v}\sum^{v(\beta)+1}_{i=v(\mathfrak{m})}\left|\varpi_{v}\right|_{v}\boldsymbol{\omega}^{k}(\varpi_{v}^{i})\left|\varpi_{v}^{i}\right|_{v}^{1-k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum^{v(\beta)}_{i=v(\mathfrak{m})}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{i}-{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}^{-1}\sum^{v(\beta\mathfrak{l})}_{i=v(\mathfrak{m})}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\mathfrak{l}}^{i}.

Combined the above formulas, we deduce that

\displaystyle C({\mathfrak{a}},{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1))

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}\subset{\mathfrak{b}}\subset\mathfrak{m},\,(p,{\mathfrak{b}})=1}{\mathrm{N}}{\mathfrak{b}}^{-1}\left<{{\mathfrak{b}}}\right>^{k}=\frac{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}C({\mathfrak{a}},\nu_{k}({\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},1)),

\displaystyle C({\mathfrak{a}},{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0}))

\displaystyle=C({\mathfrak{a}},\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}(1-{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}^{-1}U_{\mathfrak{l}}){\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1)).

We next investigate the constant terms. An elementary calculation shows that

\displaystyle f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s}(\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})=

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle Mf_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s}(\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{c}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})\bigg|_{s=\frac{1-k}{2}}=

\displaystyle\frac{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}{2^{d}{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\cdot\zeta_{F}(1-k)\prod_{{\mathfrak{p}}\mid p}(1-{\mathrm{N}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{k-1})\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1)}.

We obtain
C_{i}(0,{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0}))
equals

C({\mathfrak{a}},\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}(1-{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}^{-1}U_{\mathfrak{l}}){\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1))=\zeta_{F,p}(1-k)\cdot\frac{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}{2^{d}{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1)}.

In particular,
C_{i}(0,{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1))=\frac{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}C_{i}(0,\nu_{k}({\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},1))
.
Combining everything together, we conclude that

\displaystyle{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1))

\displaystyle=\frac{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\nu_{k}({\mathcal{E}}(\mathfrak{m},1)),

\displaystyle{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\left(1-{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{l}^{-1}U_{\mathfrak{l}}\right){\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},1).

The proposition follows.
∎

7.3.
Whittaker functions

Let
\pi
be a irreducible automorphic representation of
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
with the conductor
\mathfrak{n}
.
For each place
v
, denote by
{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})={\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{F_{v}})
the Whittaker model of
\pi_{v}
with respect to
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{F_{v}}
. Recall that this is a space of smooth functions
W
on
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F_{v})
satisfying the following properties:

•

{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})
is invariant for the right translation
\rho
of
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F_{v})
and the resulting representation is isomorphic to
\pi_{v}
;

•

For each
W\in{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})
,

W(\begin{pmatrix}{1}&{x}\\
{0}&{1}\end{pmatrix}g)=\boldsymbol{\psi}_{F_{v}}(x)W(g)\quad x\in F_{v},\,g\in\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F_{v}).

If
v\in\Sigma
and
\pi_{v}
is a discrete series of weight
k_{v}
, let
W_{\pi_{v}}\in{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})
be the Whittaker function of minimal weight
k_{v}
, i.e. the unique element in
{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})
such that

W_{\pi_{v}}(\begin{pmatrix}{y}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})=y^{\frac{k_{v}}{2}}e^{-2\pi y}\mathbb{I}_{\mathbf{R}_{+}}(y).

If
v
is a finite place of
F
, let
W_{\pi_{v}}
be the new Whittaker function in
{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v},\boldsymbol{\psi}_{F_{v}})
, i.e.
W_{\pi_{v}}
is the unique Whittaker function right invariant by
U_{1}(\mathfrak{n})
with
W_{\pi_{v}}(1)=1
. If
\pi_{v}
is a principal series and
\alpha:F_{v}^{\times}\to\mathbf{C}^{\times}
is a character, we let
W_{\pi_{v}}^{(\alpha)}
be the unique Whittaker function in
{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})
such that

W_{\pi_{v}}^{(\alpha)}(\begin{pmatrix}{a}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})=\alpha(a)\left|a\right|_{v}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(a).

7.4.
The bilinear pairing

Let
\mathbf{f}\in S_{k_{1}}(\mathfrak{n}p,\omega)
be a
p
-stabilized cuspidal Hilbert newform of weight
k_{1}\Sigma
. Let
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
be the cuspidal automorphic representation generated by
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}
. Let
\mathfrak{n}
be the conductor of
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
. Assume that

(p,\mathfrak{n}\Delta_{F})=1.

For each
v\mid p
, let
\pi_{\mathbf{f},v}=\pi(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v},\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})
be a principal series associated with unramified characters
\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v},\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}:F_{v}^{\times}\to\mathbf{C}^{\times}
. The Whiattker function
W_{\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}}
has a factorization

(7.4)

W_{\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}}=\prod_{v\mid p}W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}}\prod_{v\mid\infty}W_{\pi_{v}}\prod_{v\nmid p\infty}W_{\pi_{v}}\quad(\pi_{v}=\pi_{\mathbf{f},v}).

Put

\displaystyle S_{\rm vr}=

\displaystyle\left\{v:\text{places dividing }\mathfrak{n}\mid L(s,\pi_{\mathbf{f},v})=1\right\}.

Denote by
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}^{\vee}
the contragredient of
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
. Let
\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}
be the automorphic form in
\pi_{\mathbf{f}}^{\vee}
with the Whittaker function given by

(7.5)

W_{\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}}=\prod_{v\mid p}W^{(\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}\cdot\prod_{v\mid\infty}W_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}\prod_{v\nmid p\infty}W_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}\quad(\pi^{\vee}_{v}=\pi_{\mathbf{f},v}^{\vee}).

Note that
W^{(\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}=W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}^{-1}
. Define the
\mathbf{C}
-linear pairing
\langle\,,\,\rangle:{\mathcal{A}}^{0}_{-k_{1}}(\omega)\times{\mathcal{A}}_{k_{1}}(\omega^{-1})\to\mathbf{C}
by

(7.6)

\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(F)\backslash\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})}\varphi_{1}(g)\varphi_{2}(g)\mathrm{d}^{{\rm t}}g.

Here
\mathrm{d}^{\rm t}g
is the Tamagawa measure of
\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
. Let
n
be a positive integer. Put

\mathcal{J}_{\infty}=\begin{pmatrix}{-1}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F\otimes\mathbf{R});\quad t_{n}=\begin{pmatrix}{0}&{p^{-n}}\\
{-p^{n}}&{0}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(F\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{p}).

Proposition 7.2
.

We have

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{2L(1,\pi_{\mathbf{f}},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}{\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}\zeta_{F}(2)}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{(k_{1}+1)[F:\mathbf{Q}]}}\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{n}}\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{v\in S_{\rm vr}}\frac{1}{L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}\prod_{v\mid p}\frac{\omega_{v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{2}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}(p^{n})\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\cdot{\rm E}_{v}(\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits\mathbf{f}),

where
{\rm E}_{p}(\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits\mathbf{f})
is the modified
p
-Euler factor for the adjoint of
\mathbf{f}
given by

\displaystyle{\rm E}_{v}(\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits\mathbf{f})=

\displaystyle L(1,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})^{-1}L(0,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}(p)\left|p\right|_{v})(1-\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}(p)).

Proof.
.

For each place
v
, let
\langle-,-\rangle:{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v})\times{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{v}^{\vee})\to\mathbf{C}
be he pairing defined by

\langle W_{1},W_{2}\rangle=\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}W_{1}(\begin{pmatrix}{a_{v}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})W_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}{-a_{v}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})\mathrm{d}^{\times}a_{v}^{\rm st},

where
\mathrm{d}^{\times}a_{v}^{\rm st}
is the Haar measure on
F_{v}^{\times}
with
\mathop{\rm vol}\nolimits(\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times},\mathrm{d}^{\times}a_{v}^{\rm st})=1
. It follows from
[
Wal85
, Proposition 6]
that

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2L(1,\pi,\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}{\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}\zeta_{F}(2)}\cdot 2^{-(k_{1}+1)[F:\mathbf{Q}]}\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{n}}\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)\langle W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}\rangle}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{v\mid p}\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)\langle\rho(t_{n})W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}},W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}^{-1}\rangle}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}.

For
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
, it is well-known that

\frac{\langle W_{\pi_{v}},W_{\pi^{\vee}_{v}}\rangle}{L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }v\not\in S_{\rm vr},\\
\frac{1}{L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}&\text{ if }v\in S_{\rm vr}.\end{cases}

The local integral at primes above
p
is computed in
[
Hsi21
, Lemma 2.8]

\langle\rho(t_{n})W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}},W^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}_{\pi_{v}}\otimes\omega_{v}^{-1}\rangle=\omega_{v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{2}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}(p^{n})\cdot\gamma(0,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})\zeta_{F_{v}}(1),

where

\gamma(0,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})=\frac{\left|\mathfrak{d}_{F}\right|_{v}^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}L(1,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}{L(0,\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

7.5.
Rankin-Selberg convolution

We keep the notation in the previous subsection. Let
\pi_{1}=\pi_{\mathbf{f}}
. Let
{\bf g}^{\circ}\in S_{k_{2}}(\mathfrak{n}p,\omega^{-1})
be a Hilbert cuspform of parallell weight
k_{2}<k_{1}
. Let
\pi_{2}
be the cuspidal automorphic representation generated by
\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}
. Assume that

(RS1)

k_{1}-k_{2}
is divisible by
p-1
;

(RS2)

for every
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
,
\pi_{2,v}
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1,v}^{\vee}
up to unramified twists.

We put

\displaystyle S_{0}=

\displaystyle\left\{v:\text{places dividing }\mathfrak{n}\mid\pi_{1,v}\text{ is minimal}\right\},

\displaystyle S_{\rm dis}=

\displaystyle\left\{v\in S_{0}\mid\pi_{1,v}\text{ is a discrete series }\right\}.

Here recall that we say
\pi_{1,v}
is minimal if the conductor of
\pi_{1,v}
is minimal among that of its twists by a character. We define

(7.7)

\mathfrak{n}=\prod_{\mathfrak{q}}\mathfrak{q}^{e_{\mathfrak{q}}},\quad\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{l}_{0}^{r}\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\not\in S_{\rm dis}}\mathfrak{q}^{e_{\mathfrak{q}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\in S_{\rm dis}}\mathfrak{q}^{\lceil\frac{e_{\mathfrak{q}}}{2}\rceil};\quad\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\prod_{\mathfrak{q}\not\in S_{0}}\mathfrak{q}.

Let
S_{p}^{\rm reg}
be a subset of
S_{p}
the set of prime factors of
p
. Suppose that the automorphic form
\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}
associated with
{\bf g}^{\circ}
admits the Whittaker function

(7.8)

W_{\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}}=\prod_{v\not\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}}W_{\pi_{2,v}}\prod_{v\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}}W_{\pi_{2,v}}^{(\alpha_{{\bf g},v})}.

Proposition 7.3
.

With the assumptions (RS1-2) and
\mathcal{D}=(k_{1}-k_{2},\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}\cdot E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-1/2})\rangle}{\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}\rangle}=\frac{(\sqrt{-1})^{(k_{1}-k_{2})[F:\mathbf{Q}]}L(s,\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})}{2L(1,\pi_{1},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{1}{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{L(1,\pi_{1,v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}{L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}\prod_{v\in S_{p}}\frac{1}{{\rm E}_{v}(\mathop{\rm ad}\nolimits\mathbf{f})L(s,\pi_{2}\otimes\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})}\prod_{v\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}}\frac{1}{L(1-s,\alpha_{{\bf g},v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}.

Proof.
.

Let
N
be the unipotent upper triangular subgroup of
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}
. For each place
v
, define the trilinear form
\Psi:{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{1,v})\times{\mathscr{W}}(\pi_{2,v})\times I({\mathbf{1}}_{v},{\mathbf{1}}_{v},s)\to\mathbf{C}
by

\Psi(W_{1},W_{2},f)=\int_{N(F_{v})\backslash\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(F_{v})}W(g_{v})W_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}{-1}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}g_{v})f(g_{v})\mathrm{d}g_{v},

where
\mathrm{d}g_{v}
is the Haar measure given by

\int_{N(F_{v})\backslash\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(F_{v})}F(g_{v})\mathrm{d}g_{v}=\int_{\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathfrak{o}_{v})}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}F(\begin{pmatrix}{a_{v}}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}u_{v})\left|a_{v}\right|^{-1}\mathrm{d}^{\times}a_{v}^{\rm st}\mathrm{d}u_{v}

with
\mathop{\rm vol}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathfrak{o}_{v}),\mathrm{d}u_{v})=1
. According to
[
Jac72
, Chapter V]
(
cf.

[
CH20
, (5.11)]
), we have the basic identity

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}\cdot E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-{\frac{1}{2}}})\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(F)\backslash\mathop{\rm PGL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})}\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}(g\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}(g)E_{\mathbf{A}}(g,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-{\frac{1}{2}}})\mathrm{d}^{{\rm t}}g

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}\zeta_{F}(2)}\prod_{v\mid\infty}\Psi(\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty})W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}s-{\frac{1}{2}}})\prod_{v\nmid p\infty}\Psi(W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-{\frac{1}{2}}})

\displaystyle\quad\times\prod_{v\mid p}\Psi(W_{\pi_{1,v}}^{\mathrm{ord}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}s-{\frac{1}{2}}}).

For each place
v
, let
L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})
be the local Rankin-Selberg
L
-function for
\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v}
. If
v\in{\bf h}
and
v\nmid p\mathfrak{n}
,

\Psi(W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-{\frac{1}{2}}})=L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})

by
[
Jac72
, Proposition 15.9]
, and if
v\mid\infty
, it is computed in
[
CH20
, Proposition 5.3]
that

\displaystyle\Psi(\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty})W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v}s-{\frac{1}{2}}})=

\displaystyle(\sqrt{-1})^{k_{1}-k_{2}}2^{-k_{1}-1}L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,\infty}).

Therefore, we find that

\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-{\frac{1}{2}}})\rangle=\frac{L(s,\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})}{\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}\zeta_{F}(2)}\left(\frac{(\sqrt{-1})^{(k_{1}-k_{2})}}{2^{k_{1}+1}}\right)^{[F:\mathbf{Q}]}\prod_{v\mid p\mathfrak{n}}\Psi_{v}(s),

where
\Psi_{v}(s)
are the local zeta integrals defined by

\displaystyle\Psi_{v}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Psi(W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-1/2})}{L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}\text{ if }v\mid\mathfrak{n},

\displaystyle\Psi_{v}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Psi(\rho(t_{n})W_{\pi_{1,v}}^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-1/2})}{L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}\text{ if }v\mid p;\,v\not\in S_{p}^{\rm reg},

\displaystyle\Psi_{v}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Psi(\rho(t_{n})W_{\pi_{1,v}}^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})},W^{(\alpha_{{\bf g},v})}_{\pi_{2},v},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-1/2})}{L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}\text{ if }v\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}.

We first consider the integrals
\Psi_{v}(s)
for
v\mid\mathfrak{n}
. Put

S_{\rm i}=\left\{v\in S_{\rm dis}\mid\pi_{1,v}\text{ is supercuspidal and }\pi_{1,v}\simeq\pi_{1,v}\otimes\tau_{F_{v^{2}}/F_{v}}\right\}.

The integrals
\Psi_{v}(s)
for
v\in S_{0}
have been evaluated in
[
CH20
, Lemma 6.3, 6.5]
, and we obtain

\Psi_{v}(s)=\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)\left|\mathfrak{m}\right|_{v}}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }v\not\in S_{\rm i},\\
(1+{\mathrm{N}}v^{-1})&\text{ if }v\in S_{\rm i}.\end{cases}

If
v\not\in S_{0}\iff v\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}
, then
\pi_{1,v}
is not minimal and
\left|\mathfrak{n}\right|_{v}=\left|\mathfrak{m}\right|_{v}
. In this case,
W_{\pi_{i,v}}(\begin{pmatrix}{a}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})=\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}^{\times}}(a)\text{ for }i=1,2
and

f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s}(u)=\mathbb{I}_{U_{0}(\mathfrak{n})_{v}}(u)\text{ for }u\in\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathfrak{o}_{v}).

A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\Psi(W_{\pi_{1,v}},W_{\pi_{2,v}},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-1/2})

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm vol}\nolimits(U_{0}(\mathfrak{n})_{v},\mathrm{d}g_{v})=\frac{\left|\mathfrak{n}\right|_{v}\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)};

\displaystyle\Psi_{v}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\mathfrak{m}\right|_{v}\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}.

We next compute the local zeta integral
\Psi_{v}(s)
for
v\in S_{p}
by a similar calculation in
[
CH20
, Lemma 6.1]
. Put
W_{1}=W_{\pi_{1,v}}^{(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}
and
W_{2}=W_{\pi_{2,v}}
if
v\not\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}
or
W_{\pi_{2,v}}^{(\alpha_{{\bf g},v})}
if
v\in S_{p}^{\rm reg}
. Then
\Psi(\rho(t_{n})W_{1},W_{2},f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-1/2})
equals

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}\int_{F_{v}}W_{1}(\begin{pmatrix}{y}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{0}&{-1}\\
{1}&{x}\end{pmatrix}t_{n})W_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}{-y}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{0}&{-1}\\
{1}&{x}\end{pmatrix})\left|y\right|_{v}^{s-1}

\displaystyle\times f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D},v},s-{\frac{1}{2}}}(\begin{pmatrix}{0}&{-1}\\
{1}&{x}\end{pmatrix})\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}^{\times}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\int_{F_{v}}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}W_{1}(\begin{pmatrix}{yp^{n}}&0\\
0&{p^{-n}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{-p^{2n}x}&{1}\end{pmatrix})W_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}{-y}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{0}&{-1}\\
{1}&{x}\end{pmatrix})\left|y\right|_{v}^{s-1}

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{I}_{\mathfrak{o}_{v}}(x)\mathrm{d}^{\times}y\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}^{\frac{1}{2}}(p^{2n})\omega_{v}^{-1}(p^{n})}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\frac{1}{\gamma(s,\pi_{2}\otimes\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})}\int_{F_{v}^{\times}}W_{2}(\begin{pmatrix}{-y}&0\\
0&{1}\end{pmatrix})\omega_{2}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}^{{\frac{1}{2}}-s}(y)\mathrm{d}^{\times}y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{2}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}(p^{n})\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\cdot L(s,\pi_{2,p}\otimes\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v})\begin{cases}\frac{1}{L(1-s,\alpha_{{\bf g},v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}&\text{ if }v\in\Sigma^{\rm reg}_{p},\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here we have used the local functional equation for
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits(2)
in the second equality. Combining these formulas, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}\cdot E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-1/2})\rangle=\frac{L(s,\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2})}{\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}\zeta_{F}(2)}\left(\frac{(\sqrt{-1})^{k}}{2^{(k_{1}+1)}}\right)^{[F:\mathbf{Q}]}(\sqrt{-1})^{k_{1}-k_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{n}}\frac{\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\prod_{v\in S_{\rm i}}(1+{\mathrm{N}}v^{-1})\prod_{v\mid\mathfrak{m}_{0}}\frac{1}{L(s,\pi_{1,v}\times\pi_{2,v})}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{v\mid p}\frac{\omega_{v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{2}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{v}(p^{n})\zeta_{F_{v}}(2)}{\zeta_{F_{v}}(1)}\cdot\frac{1}{L(s,\pi_{2,p}\otimes\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})}\prod_{v\in S_{\rm reg}}\frac{1}{L(1-s,\alpha_{{\bf g},v}^{-1}\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}^{-1})}.

The result follows from Proposition
7.2
and the fact that
if
v\in S_{\rm vr}\cap S_{0}
, then
v
must be supercuspidal, and hence

L(1,\pi_{v},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)=\begin{cases}(1+{\mathrm{N}}v^{-1})^{-1}&\text{ if }v\in S_{\rm i}\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

by
[
GJ78
, Corollary (1.3)]
.
∎

7.6.
Proof of Proposition
5.3

Now we are in a position to to prove Proposition
5.3
by Hida’s
p
-adic Rankin-Selberg method. We shall use the representation theoretic approach in
[
CH20
]
. Let
d=[F:\mathbf{Q}]
. It suffices to show that for all but finitely many positive integer
k
with
k\equiv 0\,(\mbox{mod }{p^{d}-1})
,

(7.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{k}(\mathcal{C}(\phi^{c},\phi))=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}\cdot\frac{2^{d}{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,0,{\mathbf{1}}){\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,0,\chi)}{h_{K/F}\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,-k,\chi)\zeta_{F,p}(1-s)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\psi(\mathfrak{f}\mathfrak{d}_{K}^{2})^{-k}}{\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}^{\rm irr}}(1-\psi^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))^{2}\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}^{\rm reg}}(1-\psi^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))}\cdot{\rm Ex}_{\mathfrak{l}_{0}}(k).

Here recall that
\chi=\phi^{1-c}
is an anticyclotomic character of conductor
\mathfrak{f}\mathfrak{o}_{K}
and
\psi
is the idele class character of
K
corresponding to
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}
with
\psi_{\infty}(z)=z
. Note that
\psi^{k}
is unramified everywhere for
k
is divisible by
p^{d}-1
. To evaluate
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{k}(\mathcal{C}(\phi^{c},\phi))
, we consider the spectral decomposition

(7.10)

\displaystyle e_{\mathrm{ord}}(\theta_{\phi}^{\circ}\nu_{k}({\mathcal{G}}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l}_{0})))=\frac{2^{d}{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}\cdot\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}_{0}}}(1)}{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}\zeta_{F,p}(1-k)}\cdot e_{\mathrm{ord}}(\theta_{\phi}^{\circ}{\bf E}_{k}(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi,\phi^{c})\cdot\theta_{\phi\psi^{-k}}^{(\Sigma_{p})}+\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi^{c},\phi)\cdot\theta_{\phi^{c}\psi^{-k}}^{(\Sigma_{p})}+\mathscr{H}_{k}\in S_{k+1}(\mathfrak{n}p,\phi_{+}^{-1}\tau_{K/F}),

where
\mathscr{H}_{k}
is orthogonal to the space generated by
\left\{V_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\theta_{\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}},V_{\mathfrak{a}}\theta_{\phi^{-c}\psi^{-k}}\right\}_{{\mathfrak{a}}\mid p}
under the Petersson inner product. Since
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{k}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi})=\theta_{\phi\psi^{-k}}^{(\Sigma_{p})}
is a
p
-stabilized newform of weight
(k+1)\Sigma
, the decomposition (
7.10
) is indeed obtained by the image of (
5.12
) under the map
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{k}
, and hence

\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{k}(\mathcal{C}(\phi^{c},\phi))=\iota_{p}^{-1}(\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi^{c},\phi)).

Now we use the adelic Rankin-Selberg method to compute the value
\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi^{c},\phi)
.
Let
\mathbf{f}^{\rm new}=\theta_{\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}}
and
{\bf g}^{\rm new}=\theta_{\phi}
be the Hilbert newforms associated with Hecke characters
\phi^{-1}\psi^{-1}
and
\phi
. Let
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
be the cuspidal automorphic representation of
\mathop{\rm GL}\nolimits_{2}(\mathbf{A}_{F})
associated with
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}^{\rm new}}
and
\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\rm new}}
. Then
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are the automorphic inductions of the idele class characters
\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}{|\!\cdot\!|}_{\mathbf{A}_{K}}^{\frac{k}{2}}
and
\phi
, so we see that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
satisfy (RS1) and (RS2).
In addition, the automorphic
L
-functions of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are given by

\displaystyle L(s,\pi_{1})=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{C}}(s+k)^{d}D(s+\frac{k}{2},\mathbf{f}^{\rm new})=L(s+\frac{k}{2},\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k});

\displaystyle L(s,\pi_{2})=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathbf{C}}(s)^{d}D(s,{\bf g}^{\rm new})=L(s,\phi).

Let
\mathbf{f}=\theta_{\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}}^{(\Sigma_{p})}
be the
p
-stabilized newform associated with
\mathbf{f}^{\rm new}
and let
\breve{\mathbf{f}}:=\theta_{\phi^{c}\psi^{-k}}^{(\Sigma_{p})}
be the specialization of
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{\Sigma}^{s}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\phi^{c}})
at
s=k
. Let
{\bf g}^{\circ}=\theta_{\phi}^{\circ}
be the theta series defined in (
5.10
).
Let
v\mid p
with
v=\mathfrak{P}\overline{\mathfrak{P}}
and
\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}
. Then
\pi_{1,v}=\pi(\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v},\beta_{\mathbf{f},v})
, where
\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v},\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}:F_{v}^{\times}\to\mathbf{C}^{\times}
are the unramified characters such that

\alpha_{\mathbf{f},v}(p)=\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-\frac{k}{2}};\quad\beta_{\mathbf{f},v}(p)=\phi^{-1}\psi^{-k}(\mathfrak{P}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-\frac{k}{2}}.

Then one verifies that the Whitaker functions of
\varphi_{\mathbf{f}}
and
\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}
are given by (
7.4
) and (
7.5
). Likewise,
\pi_{2,v}\simeq\pi(\alpha_{{\bf g},v},\beta_{{\bf g},v})
, where
\alpha_{{\bf g},v}
and
\beta_{{\bf g},v}
are unramified characters of
F_{v}^{\times}
with
\alpha_{{\bf g},v}(p)=\phi(\mathfrak{P})
and
\beta_{{\bf g},v}(p)=\phi(\overline{\mathfrak{P}})
. Then one verifies that the Whittaker function of
\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}
is precisely given by (
7.8
). In the notation of previous two subsections,

•

k_{1}=k+1
and
k_{2}=1
.

•

S_{\rm dis}
is the set of prime factors of
{\mathfrak{c}}^{-}
and
e_{\mathfrak{q}}
is even for
\mathfrak{q}\in S_{\rm dis}
.

•

S_{0}
is the set of prime factors of
\mathfrak{n}
except for
\mathfrak{l}
, and
\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\mathfrak{l}
in view of (
min
).

•

The ideal
\mathfrak{m}
in (
5.8
) is the one introduced in (
7.7
).

By
[
CH20
, Proposition 5.2]
, for
n\gg 0
large enough, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi^{c},\phi)=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{{\bf g}^{\circ}}\cdot E_{\mathbf{A}}(-,f_{\Phi_{\mathcal{D}},s-1/2})\rangle|_{s=1-\frac{k}{2}}}{\langle\rho(\mathcal{J}_{\infty}t_{n})\varphi_{\mathbf{f}},\varphi_{\breve{\mathbf{f}}}\rangle}\cdot\frac{2^{d}{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1)}{\zeta_{F,p}(1-k)\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}.

Note that by definition

{\rm Ex}_{\mathfrak{l}}(k)=\frac{\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1)^{2}L(1,\phi^{1-c}\psi^{(1-c)k})}{L(1,\psi^{-k})L(1,\phi^{c-1}\psi^{-k})}=\frac{\zeta_{F_{\mathfrak{l}}}(1)L(1,\pi_{1,\mathfrak{l}},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}{L(1-\frac{k}{2},\pi_{1,\mathfrak{l}}\times\pi_{2,\mathfrak{l}})}.

Put
\psi_{-}=\psi^{1-c}
. From Proposition
7.2
and Proposition
7.3
we deduce that

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k}(\phi^{c},\phi)=\frac{1}{\left<{{\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{m}}\right>^{k}}\cdot\frac{2^{d}(\sqrt{-1})^{k\Sigma}L(1,\psi^{-k})L(1,\phi^{c-1}\psi^{-k})}{2L(1,\pi_{1},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)\zeta_{F,p}(1-k)}

\displaystyle\times\frac{\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\psi^{-k}(\mathfrak{P}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-1})(1-\phi^{c-1}\psi^{-k}(\mathfrak{P}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-1})\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}^{\rm reg}}(1-\phi^{1-c}\psi^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))}{\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}(1-\chi\psi_{-}^{k}(\mathfrak{P})^{-1}p^{-1})(1-\chi\psi_{-}^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))}

\displaystyle\times{\rm Ex}_{\mathfrak{l}}(k).

By the functional equations of
L
-functions, one has

\displaystyle L(1,\psi^{-k})L(1,\phi^{c-1}\psi^{-k})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(1,\psi^{-k})\varepsilon(1,\chi^{-1}\psi^{-k})L(0,\psi^{k})L(0,\chi\psi^{k}),

\displaystyle L(1,\pi_{1},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)

\displaystyle=L(1,\tau_{K/F})L(1,\phi^{1-c}\psi^{(1-c)k})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(1,\chi\psi_{-}^{k})\cdot L(1,\tau_{K/\mathbf{Q}})L(0,\chi\psi_{-}^{k}).

Note that
\mathfrak{f}\mathfrak{o}_{K}
is the conductor of
\chi
and
\psi^{k}
is unramified everywhere with
\psi_{-}|_{\mathbf{A}^{\times}_{F}}=1
, so we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon(1,\psi^{-k})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Delta_{K}}^{-1}\psi(\mathfrak{d}_{K})^{-k},\quad\varepsilon(1,\chi\psi^{-k})=\varepsilon(1,\chi)\psi(\mathfrak{f}\mathfrak{d}_{K})^{-k},

\displaystyle\varepsilon(1,\chi\psi_{-}^{k})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(1,\chi).

It follows that

(7.12)

\frac{(\sqrt{-1})^{k\Sigma}L(1,\psi^{-k})L(1,\phi^{c-1}\psi^{-k})}{2L(1,\pi_{1},\mathop{\rm Ad}\nolimits)}=\frac{(\sqrt{-1})^{k\Sigma}L(0,\psi^{k})L(0,\chi\psi^{k})}{2\sqrt{\Delta_{K}}L(1,\tau_{K/\mathbf{Q}})L(0,\chi\psi_{-}^{k})}\cdot\psi(\mathfrak{f}\mathfrak{d}_{K}^{2})^{-k}.

By the interpolation formulae of the Katz
p
-adic
L
-function in (
4.14
), we find that

(7.13)

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,0,\chi){\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,0,{\mathbf{1}})}{{\mathcal{L}}_{\Sigma}(k,-k,\chi)}=

\displaystyle\frac{[\mathfrak{o}_{K}^{\times}:\mathfrak{o}_{F}^{\times}]}{2^{d}\sqrt{\Delta_{F}}(\sqrt{-1})^{k\Sigma}}\frac{L(0,\chi\psi^{k})L(0,\psi^{k})}{L(0,\chi\psi_{-}^{k})}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathfrak{P}\in\Sigma_{p}}\frac{(1-\psi^{k}(\mathfrak{P}^{-1}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-1})(1-\chi\psi^{k}(\mathfrak{P}^{-1}){\mathrm{N}}\mathfrak{P}^{-1})(1-\psi^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))^{2}}{(1-\chi\psi_{-}^{k}(\mathfrak{P}^{-1})p^{-1})(1-\chi\psi_{-}^{k}(\overline{\mathfrak{P}}))}.

Combining (
7.11
), (
7.12
), (
7.13
) and (
4.8
),
we obtain (
7.9
). This finishes the proof.

Appendix A
Transcendental number theory and non-vanishing of the
p
-adic regulators

A.1.
The
p
-adic transcendence conjectures

The non-vanishing of the regulators are usually tied with well-known conjectures in
p
-adic transcendental number theory. We first recall some standard results and conjectures in
p
-adic transcendence.
As before,
\log_{p}:\overline{\mathbf{Q}}{}_{p}^{\times}\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
is the Iwasawa
p
-adic logarithm with
\log_{p}(p)=0
, and we use the same notation for its composition with
\iota_{p}:\overline{\mathbf{Q}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
. Denote
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}\subset\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
the
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
-vector space generated by
1
and elements of

\log_{p}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}{}^{\times})
. We recall the statements of two conjectures at the top of a Hierarchy of conjectures and theorems concerning the arithmetic nature of certain values of
\log_{p}
.

Conjecture 5

(Strong Four Exponentials Conjecture)
.

Let
M
be a
2\times 2
matrix
with entries in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
. Assume the rows and the columns of
M
are linearly independent over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively. Then
M
has rank
2
.

Conjecture 6

(
p
-adic Schanuel’s Conjecture)
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\overline{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}
such that

\log_{p}(\alpha_{1}),\dots,\log_{p}(\alpha_{n})

are linearly independent over
\mathbf{Q}
. Then
\mathbf{Q}(\log_{p}(\alpha_{1}),\dots,\log_{p}(\alpha_{n}))
has transcendence degree
n
over
\mathbf{Q}
.

Remark A.1
.

The significance of thse
p
-adic transcendental conjectures perhaps stems from their direct applications to the non-vanishing of
p
-adic regulators. Below we give some examples without details.

(1)

If
F
is a real quadratic field, the Strong Four Exponentials conjecture implies that
\mathscr{R}_{\Sigma}(\chi)\neq 0
, i.e. the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture for
\chi
holds.

(2)

The
p
-adic
Schanuel conjecture implies the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture (
cf.

[
HT94
, Lemma 1.2.1]
) and the non-vanishing of the
\mathscr{L}
-invariants
\mathscr{L}_{\chi}
and
\mathscr{L}_{\chi}^{\rm ac}
.

A.2.
Examples of the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture

Notation is as in the introduction. Recall that
\chi
is a ray class character of a CM field
K
and
H
is the abelian extension over
K
cut out by
\chi
. We give examples of
(\chi,K,\Sigma,p)
such that
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture for
\chi
holds,
i.e.,
Conjecture
2
(or equivalently (

Σ

-Leo
) holds). The following famous Baker-Brumer Theorem is the best result towards these conjectures in the current literature.

Theorem A.2

(Baker–Brumer
[
Bru67
]
)
.

Let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in\mathcal{L}
are linearly independent over
\mathbf{Q}
, then they are linearly independent over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Corollary A.3
.

Suppose that
K=FM
for an imaginary quadratic field
M
in which
p
splits and
H/M
is abelian. Suppose also that the CM type
\Sigma
of
K
is obtained by extending the CM type of
M
, i.e. a choice of embeddings
\iota:M\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}
. Then the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture for
\chi:\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/K)\to\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
holds.

Proof.
.

By the Dirichlet unit theorem (
cf.

[
NSW08
, Prop. 8.7.2, p.503]
), there is an isomorphism

\mathbf{Q}\oplus\mathbf{Q}\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}\simeq\mathbf{Q}[\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)]

as
\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)
-modules, so there exists a
Minkowski
unit
u\in\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}
, i.e. the set
\left\{\sigma(u)u^{-1}\right\}_{\sigma\in\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)\backslash\left\{1\right\}}
is a basis of
\mathbf{Q}\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}
.
Moreover, since
H/M
is abelian and
\chi\neq 1
, we find that

{\rm H}^{1}_{(f,f)}(K,\chi^{-1}(1))\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{d}\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi_{i}]\text{ as $\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)$-modules},

where
\chi_{i}
are characters of
\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)
extending
\chi
and that
\dim_{E}\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi_{i}]=1
. For any character
\chi_{i}\neq{\mathbf{1}}
,

u_{\chi_{i}}:=\sum_{\tau\in\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/M)}\chi_{i}(\tau^{-1})\otimes\tau(u)u^{-1}\in\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi_{i}].

Then
\log_{p}(u_{\chi_{i}})\neq 0
by the Baker-Brumer Theorem. It follows that
\log_{p}:\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi_{i}]\simeq\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
is an isomorphism and
\log_{\Sigma,p}:{\rm H}^{1}_{(f,f)}(K,\chi^{-1}(1))\simeq\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{d}
.
∎

In the above example,
H
is abelian over an imaginary quadratic field
M
, so the Katz
p
-adic
L
-function
L_{\Sigma}(s,\chi)
is a product of
p
-adic
L
-functions over
M
, and the trivial zeros in this case have been studied extensively in
[
BS19
]
via the Euler system of elliptic units. To obtain more interesting examples beyond this case, we will make use of the following strong version of the Six Exponentials Theorem due to Roy
[
Roy92
, Corollary 2]

Theorem A.4

(Strong Six Exponentials Theorem)
.

Let
M
be a
2\times 3
matrix
with entries in
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
. Assume the rows and the columns of
M
are linearly independent over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively. Then
M
has rank
2
.

Proposition A.5
.

Assume that
F
is a quadratic real field in which
p
splits and
\chi\neq\chi^{c}
. Then there exists a
p
-adic CM type
\Sigma
of
K
such that the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture holds.

Proof.
.

We let
L
be the Galois closure of
H
. Choose a
p
-adic CM type
\Sigma=\left\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\right\}
of
K/F
and let
\Sigma_{L,i}
be the subset of
\sigma\in\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L/\mathbf{Q})
such that
\sigma|_{K}=\sigma_{i}
. Let
G_{\infty}=\left\{1,c\right\}\subset\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L/\mathbf{Q})
be the decomposition at the archimdean place induced by
\iota_{\infty}:\overline{\mathbf{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}
. By the Dirichlet unit Theorem, there is an isomorphism as
\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L/\mathbf{Q})
-modules

\mathbf{Q}\oplus\mathbf{Q}\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}\simeq\mathop{\rm Ind}\nolimits_{G_{\infty}}^{\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(L/\mathbf{Q})}\mathbf{Q}.

It follows that there exists
\mathbf{u}\in\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\times}
such that

\left\{\tau(\mathbf{u})\mathbf{u}^{-1}\right\}_{\tau\in\Sigma_{L,1}\sqcup\Sigma_{L.2}}
is a basis of
\mathbf{Q}\mathfrak{o}_{L}^{\times}
. For
i=1,2
, put
\mathbf{u}_{i}=\prod_{\sigma\in\Sigma_{L,i}}\sigma(\mathbf{u})\in\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}
and

u_{i}:=e_{\chi}\mathbf{u}_{i}=\sum_{\tau\in\mathop{\rm Gal}\nolimits(H/K)}\chi(\tau^{-1})\otimes\tau(\mathbf{u}_{i})\mathbf{u}_{i}^{-1}\in\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi].

Then
\left\{u_{1},u_{2}\right\}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{o}_{H}^{\times}[\chi]
. Consider the matrix

M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{1}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}(u_{1}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}c(u_{1}))\\
\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{2}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}(u_{2}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}c(u_{2}))\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Since
\chi\neq\chi^{c}
, we see that the row and the column vectors are also linearly independent over
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}
respectively by the Baker-Brumer theorem. According to Roy’s Six exponentials theorem,
M
has rank
2
. Therefore either
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{1}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}(u_{1}))\\
\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{2}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}(u_{2}))\end{smallmatrix}\right)
or
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{1}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}c(u_{1}))\\
\log_{p}(\sigma_{1}(u_{2}))&\log_{p}(\sigma_{2}c(u_{2}))\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is invertible. This shows that either
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}
or
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}c\}
is a
p
-adic CM type that satisfies the
\Sigma
-Leopoldt conjecture for
\chi
.
∎
